# CHAPTER 3: ALGORITHMS AND PROBLEM SOLVING

## A. Chapter Introduction

**Chapter Overview:** This chapter teaches you how to think like a computer scientist. You will learn what computational problems are, how to classify them, and how to design step-by-step solutions (algorithms). You will also learn why some problems are easy to solve while others are extremely difficult or even impossible, and you will study specific algorithms for sorting, searching, and traversing graphs.

**Learning Objectives (from textbook SLOs):**
- Describe and categorize different types of computational problems
- Explain the importance of algorithms in problem-solving
- Apply the generate-and-test method
- Differentiate between solvable and unsolvable problems
- Understand problem complexity and categorize problems into P, NP, NP-Hard, NP-Complete
- Identify common computational problems like sorting and searching
- Apply algorithm design techniques (divide and conquer, greedy, dynamic programming)
- Implement and compare algorithms (Bubble Sort, Binary Search, BFS, DFS)
- Evaluate algorithms in terms of efficiency and scalability
- Develop algorithmic thinking

**Why This Chapter Is Important:** Every computer program is built on algorithms. Understanding algorithms helps you write efficient code, solve complex problems, and understand the limits of what computers can do. This knowledge is essential for programming, data science, and software engineering.

**Roman Urdu:** Yeh chapter sikhaata hai ke computer problems ko kaise solve karte hain. Algorithm matlab step-by-step tareeqa. Kuch problems asaan hain, kuch mushkil, aur kuch impossible. Sorting, searching, aur graph algorithms seekh kar aap efficient programs likh sakte hain.

**Real-Life Applications:** Google Search uses algorithms to rank pages. GPS uses graph algorithms to find shortest routes. Sorting algorithms are used in databases, e-commerce, and data analysis.

---

## B. Complete Topic-Wise Notes

### 3.1 Understanding Computational Problems

**Definition:** A computational problem is a challenge that can be solved through a computational process using an algorithm.

**Easy Explanation:** A computational problem is any problem a computer can help solve. It has three parts: what you start with (input), what you do (process), and what you get (output).

**Roman Urdu:** Computational problem wo masla hai jise computer ki madad se solve kiya ja sakta hai. Iske teen hisse hote hain: input (kya diya gaya), process (kya karna hai), aur output (kya result mila).

**Three Components:**
- **Input:** The data or information given to the algorithm at the beginning
- **Process:** The steps or rules (algorithm) applied to the input
- **Output:** The solution or result produced

**Roman Urdu:** Input wo data hai jo shuru mein diya jata hai. Process wo steps hain jo apply hote hain. Output wo result hai jo milta hai.

**Example:** Finding the largest number in a list.
- Input: A list of numbers [5, 2, 9, 1, 7]
- Process: Compare each number, keep track of the largest
- Output: 9

**Roman Urdu:** List mein sab se bada number dhundhna. Input list hai, process compare karna hai, output sab se bada number hai.

---

#### 3.1.1 Characterizing Computational Problems

**Definition:** To solve a problem computationally, we need to understand its characteristics — inputs, desired outputs, and the process to transform inputs into outputs.

**Roman Urdu:** Problem ko solve karne ke liye pehle uske inputs, outputs, aur process ko samajhna zaroori hai.

##### 3.1.1.1 Classifying Computational Problems

Computational problems are classified into different categories based on their characteristics:

**1. Decision Problems**
- Output is a simple "yes" or "no"
- Example: Is a number even? Is a string a palindrome?
- Roman Urdu: Decision problems ka jawab sirf "haan" ya "nahi" hota. Jaise: kya yeh number even hai?

**2. Search Problems**
- Task is to find a solution or an item that meets certain criteria
- Example: Find a specific student in a database, find the shortest path between two cities
- Roman Urdu: Search problems mein koi cheez dhundhni hoti hai jo criteria poori kare. Jaise database mein student dhundhna.

**3. Optimization Problems**
- Goal is to find the best solution according to some criteria
- Example: Find the shortest route, maximize profit, minimize cost
- Roman Urdu: Optimization problems mein best solution dhundhna hota hai. Jaise sab se chhota raasta ya sab se zyada profit.

**4. Counting Problems**
- Objective is to count the number of ways certain conditions can be met
- Example: How many ways can you arrange 5 books on a shelf? How many passwords are possible?
- Roman Urdu: Counting problems mein yeh gina jata hai ke kitne tareeqe se koi kaam ho sakta hai. Jaise 5 kitaabon ko kitne tareeqe se rakh sakte hain.

**Comparison Table:**

| Problem Type | Goal | Example | Roman Urdu |
|--------------|------|---------|------------|
| Decision | Yes/No answer | Is number even? | Haan ya nahi |
| Search | Find an item | Find student by ID | Item dhundhna |
| Optimization | Find best solution | Shortest path | Best solution |
| Counting | Count possibilities | Number of arrangements | Kitne tareeqe |

##### 3.1.1.2 Well-defined vs. Ill-defined Problems

**Well-defined Problems:**
- Clear goals, inputs, processes, and outputs
- No ambiguity
- Can be solved by an algorithm

**Example:** Determining if a number is even.
- Goal: Determine if even
- Input: A single integer
- Process: Check if divisible by 2
- Output: Even or odd

**Roman Urdu:** Well-defined problems mein sab kuch clear hota hai — goal, input, process, output. Jaise even number check karna.

**Ill-defined Problems:**
- Lack clear definitions
- May have ambiguous goals and requirements
- Difficult to solve computationally

**Example:** "How to reduce poverty in Pakistan."
- Goal is vague and broad
- No clear input, process, or output
- Many possible interpretations

**Roman Urdu:** Ill-defined problems mein goal clear nahi hota. Jaise "Pakistan mein ghurbat kam karna" — yeh bohat broad hai, computer se solve nahi ho sakta.

**Comparison:**

| Feature | Well-defined | Ill-defined |
|---------|-------------|-------------|
| Goal | Clear | Vague |
| Input | Defined | Unclear |
| Process | Known | Unknown |
| Output | Specific | Ambiguous |
| Solvable by computer | Yes | No (or difficult) |
| Roman Urdu | Sab clear | Kuch clear nahi |

**Figure 3.1: Finding an Even Number** — Shows the process: Start → Input number → Check if divisible by 2 → Yes/No → Output Even/Odd.

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 3.1 mein even number dhundhne ka process dikhaya hai. Number lo, 2 se divide karo, agar remainder 0 ho to even, warna odd.

---

### 3.2 Algorithms for Problem Solving

**Definition:** Algorithms are step-by-step procedures for solving problems, much like a recipe provides steps for cooking a dish.

**Easy Explanation:** An algorithm is a precise set of instructions. If you follow them exactly, you will solve the problem. It is like a recipe — follow the steps, get the dish.

**Roman Urdu:** Algorithm step-by-step instructions hain jo problem solve karti hain. Jaise recipe follow karke khana banate hain, waise algorithm follow karke problem solve karte hain.

**Why Algorithms Are Important:**
- Provide the logic behind software operations
- Help solve complex problems
- Optimize performance
- Ensure accuracy

**Roman Urdu:** Algorithms software ka logic hain. Inse complex problems solve hoti hain, performance behtar hoti hai, aur accuracy milti hai.

**Textbook "Do You Know?":** Google's PageRank algorithm determines the relevance of web pages by considering the number of links to a page and the quality of those links.

**Roman Urdu:** Google ka PageRank algorithm web pages ki relevance determine karta hai — kitne links hain aur unki quality kaisi hai.

**Textbook Tidbit:** "When learning about algorithms, try to relate them to real-life tasks you already know. This will help you understand how algorithms work and why they are important."

---

#### 3.2.1 Generate-and-Test Method

**Definition:** This method works by generating potential solutions to a problem and then testing each one to determine if it meets the required conditions. The process continues until a satisfactory solution is found or all possible solutions have been exhausted.

**Easy Explanation:** Generate-and-test means trying every possible answer until you find the right one. It is like trying every key on a keychain until one opens the lock.

**Roman Urdu:** Generate-and-test mein har possible solution try karte hain jab tak sahi na mil jaye. Jaise chaabi ke har key try karna jab tak taala na khule.

**When It Is Useful:**
- Problem space is small (few possible solutions)
- No clear strategy for finding a solution
- Heuristics can reduce the number of solutions to try

**Roman Urdu:** Jab possible solutions kam hon, ya koi clear strategy na ho, tab generate-and-test use hota hai.

**Figure 3.2: Flowchart of Generate and Test Method**
[Shows: Generator → Possible Solution → Tester → Correct Solution → Stop; Incorrect Solution → back to Generator]

**Explanation of Figure 3.2:**
- **Generator:** Creates possible solutions
- **Tester:** Checks if the solution is correct
- If correct → STOP
- If incorrect → go back to Generator for another solution

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 3.2 mein Generator possible solutions banata hai. Tester check karta hai ke sahi hai ya nahi. Agar sahi ho to stop, agar ghalat ho to dobara try karo.

**Textbook "Do You Know?":** "The Generate-and-Test method is often used in AI applications, such as game playing and problem-solving, where the solution space is large, and the best approach is to try different possibilities until one works!"

**Roman Urdu:** AI mein generate-and-test use hota hai — jaise game khelne mein different possibilities try karna.

**Example:** Finding a password by trying all possible combinations (brute force).

**Roman Urdu:** Password dhundhne ke liye har possible combination try karna — yeh generate-and-test hai.

---

### 3.3 Problem Solvability and Complexity

**Definition:** Problem solvability and complexity helps us determine whether a problem can be solved using an algorithm and, if so, how efficiently it can be solved.

**Roman Urdu:** Problem solvability aur complexity se pata chalta hai ke problem solve ho sakti hai ya nahi, aur agar ho sakti hai to kitni efficiently.

#### 3.3.1 Solvable vs. Unsolvable Problems

**Solvable Problems:**
- An algorithm can solve them within a finite amount of time
- Clearly defined inputs and outputs
- Step-by-step procedure exists

**Example:** Calculating the greatest common divisor (GCD) of two integers using the Euclidean algorithm.

**Roman Urdu:** Solvable problems wo hain jinhein algorithm finite time mein solve kar sakta hai. Jaise GCD nikalna — Euclidean algorithm se.

**Unsolvable Problems:**
- No algorithm can provide a solution for all cases
- No general procedure guarantees a solution

**Example: The Halting Problem**
- Determining whether a given program will eventually halt (finish) or run forever
- Alan Turing proved no general algorithm can solve this for all program-input pairs

**Roman Urdu:** Unsolvable problems wo hain jinhein koi algorithm solve nahi kar sakta. Halting problem famous hai — kya program rukega ya nahi, yeh pata nahi lag sakta.

**Textbook Tidbit:** "When tackling complex problems, it's essential to first determine whether the problem is solvable. This saves time and resources."

---

#### 3.3.2 Tractable vs. Intractable Problems

**Tractable Problems:**
- Can be solved in polynomial time (Class P)
- Time grows at a manageable rate
- Considered "efficiently solvable"

**Example:** Sorting a list using Merge Sort or Quick Sort — O(n log n).

**Roman Urdu:** Tractable problems polynomial time mein solve ho jate hain — time manageable hota hai. Jaise sorting.

**Intractable Problems:**
- Require super-polynomial time (often exponential)
- Impractical to solve for large inputs
- Time becomes unmanageable

**Example:** Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) — finding the shortest route visiting all cities.

**Roman Urdu:** Intractable problems exponential time lete hain — bade inputs ke liye solve karna impractical hai. Jaise TSP.

**Comparison:**

| Feature | Tractable | Intractable |
|---------|-----------|-------------|
| Time Complexity | Polynomial | Exponential or worse |
| Feasibility | Practical | Impractical for large inputs |
| Class | P | NP-Hard, etc. |
| Example | Sorting | Traveling Salesman |
| Roman Urdu | Manageable time | Bohat zyada time |

---

#### 3.3.3 Complexity Classes (P, NP, NP-hard, NP-complete)

**3.3.3.1 Class P**

**Definition:** Problems that can be solved efficiently (in polynomial time) by a deterministic algorithm.

**Easy Explanation:** Class P problems are the "easy" problems. A computer can find the solution quickly, even for large inputs.

**Example:** Sorting a list of numbers.
- Input: [5, 2, 8, 1, 9]
- Output: [1, 2, 5, 8, 9]
- Time grows at a manageable rate as list grows

**Roman Urdu:** Class P wo problems hain jinhein computer jaldi solve kar sakta hai. Jaise sorting — list bari ho jaye to bhi time manageable rehta hai.

**3.3.3.2 Class NP**

**Definition:** Problems for which, if a solution is given, it can be checked quickly (in polynomial time). Finding the solution might be difficult, but verifying it is easy.

**Easy Explanation:** NP problems are like puzzles. If someone gives you the answer, you can quickly check if it is correct. But finding the answer yourself might be hard.

**Example: Sudoku**
- Given a completed Sudoku grid, you can quickly check if it is valid (each row, column, and 3x3 box contains 1-9 exactly once)
- But solving a Sudoku from scratch can be time-consuming

**Figure 3.3: A simple Sudoku Puzzle** — Shows a 9x9 grid with some numbers filled in.

**Roman Urdu:** NP problems mein agar solution diya jaye to check karna asaan hai, lekin solution dhundhna mushkil ho sakta hai. Jaise Sudoku — solved grid check karna asaan, solve karna mushkil.

**3.3.3.3 Class NP-Hard**

**Definition:** Problems that are at least as difficult as the hardest problems in NP. No efficient algorithm is known for finding a solution.

**Example:** Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP)
- Finding the shortest route visiting all cities and returning to start
- As number of cities grows, possible routes grow exponentially

**Roman Urdu:** NP-Hard problems NP ki sab se mushkil problems se bhi mushkil hain. Inka koi efficient algorithm nahi mila. Jaise TSP.

**3.3.3.4 NP-Complete**

**Definition:** Problems that are both in NP and as hard as the hardest problems in NP. If you can solve one NP-Complete problem efficiently, you can solve all NP problems efficiently.

**Example: Knapsack Problem**
- You have a knapsack with maximum weight capacity
- Items each have weight and value
- Goal: Find most valuable combination without exceeding weight capacity

**Figure 3.4: Venn Diagram of Complexity Classes**
[Shows: P inside NP, NP-Complete at intersection of NP and NP-Hard, NP-Hard outside]

**Explanation of Figure 3.4:**
- **P:** Innermost circle (easiest problems)
- **NP:** Contains P and NP-Complete
- **NP-Hard:** Overlaps with NP at NP-Complete
- **NP-Complete:** Intersection of NP and NP-Hard

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 3.4 mein P sab se andar hai (asaan problems). NP mein P aur NP-Complete shamil hain. NP-Hard NP ke saath NP-Complete par milta hai.

**The P vs. NP Question:** One of the most important unsolved problems in computer science. It asks whether P equals NP — i.e., whether every problem whose solution can be quickly verified can also be quickly solved.

**Roman Urdu:** P vs NP sawal hai ke kya har problem jise jaldi verify kar sakte hain, usse jaldi solve bhi kar sakte hain? Yeh abhi tak unsolved hai.

**Comparison Table:**

| Class | Definition | Example | Roman Urdu |
|-------|-----------|---------|------------|
| P | Solvable in polynomial time | Sorting | Jaldi solve |
| NP | Solution verifiable in polynomial time | Sudoku | Jaldi check |
| NP-Hard | At least as hard as hardest NP | TSP | Bohat mushkil |
| NP-Complete | In NP and NP-Hard | Knapsack | Sab se mushkil NP |

---

### 3.4 Algorithm Analysis

**Definition:** Algorithm analysis is the process of determining the computational complexity of algorithms — their time and space complexity.

**Roman Urdu:** Algorithm analysis mein dekhte hain ke algorithm kitna time aur memory leta hai.

#### 3.4.1 Time Complexity

**Definition:** Time complexity measures how the running time of an algorithm increases as the size of the input data grows.

**Easy Explanation:** Time complexity tells you how much slower an algorithm gets when you give it more data.

**Roman Urdu:** Time complexity batati hai ke input bara hone par algorithm kitna slow hota hai.

**Example:** Sorting a list.
- Few numbers → quick
- Many numbers → takes longer
- Time complexity predicts how much longer

##### 3.4.1.1 Big O Notation

**Definition:** Big O notation is a mathematical way to describe the time complexity of an algorithm. It provides an upper bound on the time an algorithm will take as input size grows.

**Easy Explanation:** Big O is like a speed rating. It tells you the worst-case performance of an algorithm.

**Roman Urdu:** Big O notation algorithm ki time complexity batata hai — worst case mein kitna time lagega.

**Common Big O Notations:**

**O(1) — Constant Time**
- Runtime remains the same regardless of input size
- Example: Accessing an array element by index
- Roman Urdu: Input size se farq nahi padta — hamesha same time.

**O(n) — Linear Time**
- Runtime grows linearly with input size
- Example: Searching for a student by checking each student one by one
- Roman Urdu: Input jitna bara, time utna zyada — linear.

**O(n²) — Quadratic Time**
- Runtime increases with the square of input size
- Example: Comparing each student with every other student
- Roman Urdu: Input ka square — double input, four times time.

**O(log n) — Logarithmic Time**
- Runtime grows logarithmically (very slowly)
- Example: Binary search — each step halves the search space
- Roman Urdu: Bohat slowly badhta hai — har step mein aadha ho jata hai.

**Figure 3.5: Growth of Asymptotic Notations**
[Shows graph: O(1) flat, O(log n) slightly rising, O(n) straight line, O(n²) steep curve]

**Explanation of Figure 3.5:**
- X-axis: Input size
- Y-axis: Time
- O(1): Flat line (no change)
- O(log n): Very slow rise
- O(n): Straight diagonal
- O(n²): Steep upward curve

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 3.5 mein dikhaya hai ke input barhne par time kaise barhta hai. O(1) flat hai, O(n²) tezi se barhta hai.

**Comparison Table:**

| Notation | Name | Growth | Example | Roman Urdu |
|----------|------|--------|---------|------------|
| O(1) | Constant | None | Array access | Same time |
| O(log n) | Logarithmic | Very slow | Binary search | Bohat slow |
| O(n) | Linear | Proportional | Linear search | Linear |
| O(n²) | Quadratic | Squared | Bubble sort | Square |

---

#### 3.4.2 Space Complexity

**Definition:** Space complexity measures how the amount of memory an algorithm uses changes as the input data grows.

**Easy Explanation:** Space complexity tells you how much memory an algorithm needs.

**Roman Urdu:** Space complexity batati hai ke algorithm kitni memory leta hai.

**Example:** Storing a list of numbers — more numbers = more memory.

**Roman Urdu:** List mein zyada numbers = zyada memory.

---

### 3.5 Algorithm Design Techniques

**Definition:** Algorithm design involves creating systematic methods to solve problems efficiently.

**Roman Urdu:** Algorithm design mein systematic tareeqe se problems solve karte hain.

#### 3.5.1 Divide and Conquer

**Definition:** Breaks a large problem into smaller, manageable parts. Each part is solved independently, and solutions are combined to solve the original problem.

**Easy Explanation:** Divide and conquer is like cleaning a large house by dividing it into rooms — clean each room separately, then the whole house is clean.

**Roman Urdu:** Divide and conquer mein bari problem ko chhote hisson mein todte hain. Har hissa alag solve karte hain, phir sab ko mila dete hain. Jaise ghar ki safai — har kamra alag saaf karo.

**Steps:**
1. **Divide:** Break problem into subproblems
2. **Conquer:** Solve each subproblem
3. **Combine:** Merge solutions

**Roman Urdu:** Divide (todna), Conquer (hal karna), Combine (milana).

**Figure 3.6: Merge Sort Process** — Shows how Merge Sort divides a list and merges sorted halves.

**Example: Merge Sort**
- Divide list into halves
- Sort each half
- Merge sorted halves

**Roman Urdu:** Merge Sort mein list ko aadha karte hain, har aadha sort karte hain, phir merge karte hain.

**Textbook "Do You Know?":** "Big O notation helps computer scientists understand the efficiency of an algorithm in the worst-case scenario."

---

#### 3.5.2 Greedy Algorithms

**Definition:** Greedy algorithms make a sequence of choices, each locally optimal, hoping these choices lead to a globally optimal solution.

**Easy Explanation:** Greedy means choosing the best option right now without worrying about the future. It is like taking the largest coin first when making change.

**Roman Urdu:** Greedy algorithm har step par sab se behtar option chunta hai — abhi ke liye best, chahe future mein nuksan ho ya faida.

**Example: Coin Change Problem**
- Coins: [1, 5, 10, 25]
- Amount: 63
- Greedy: 25 + 25 + 10 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 63 (6 coins)

**Roman Urdu:** Coin change mein sab se bara coin pehle lo — 25, 25, 10, 1, 1, 1.

**Textbook Tidbit:** "Greedy algorithms are often faster and easier to implement than other techniques, but they don't always guarantee the optimal solution for every problem."

**Roman Urdu:** Greedy algorithms tez aur asaan hote hain, lekin har problem ke liye best solution guarantee nahi karte.

---

#### 3.5.3 Dynamic Programming

**Definition:** Dynamic Programming (DP) solves problems by breaking them into simpler subproblems and storing results of subproblems to avoid redundant calculations.

**Easy Explanation:** DP is like remembering answers to questions you have already solved. If the same question comes again, you do not solve it again — you just recall the answer.

**Roman Urdu:** DP mein subproblems ke results store karte hain taake dobara calculate na karna pade. Jaise purane sawalon ke jawab yaad rakhna.

**Example: Fibonacci Sequence**
- Without DP: Recalculates Fibonacci numbers repeatedly (slow)
- With DP: Stores each Fibonacci number as computed (fast)

**Roman Urdu:** Fibonacci mein DP use karte hain — har number ko ek baar calculate karke store karte hain.

**Comparison:**

| Feature | Without DP | With DP |
|---------|-----------|---------|
| Recalculation | Yes | No |
| Speed | Slow | Fast |
| Memory | Less | More (stores results) |
| Roman Urdu | Dobara calculate | Store karke reuse |

---

#### 3.5.4 Backtracking

**Definition:** Backtracking builds a solution step by step. If a path does not lead to a solution, you go back and try a different path.

**Easy Explanation:** Backtracking is like trying to solve a maze. If you hit a dead end, you go back and try another path.

**Roman Urdu:** Backtracking mein step by step solution banate hain. Agar raasta ghalat ho to wapas ja kar doosra raasta try karte hain. Jaise maze mein dead end par wapas jana.

**Example:** Solving a Sudoku puzzle — try a number, if it leads to contradiction, backtrack and try another number.

**Roman Urdu:** Sudoku mein number daalo, agar contradiction ho to wapas ja kar doosra number try karo.

---

### 3.6 Commonly Used Algorithms

#### 3.6.1 Sorting Algorithms

**Definition:** Sorting algorithms arrange data in a particular order (ascending or descending).

**Roman Urdu:** Sorting algorithms data ko order mein arrange karte hain.

##### 3.6.1.1 Bubble Sort

**Definition:** Repeatedly steps through the list, compares adjacent elements, and swaps them if they are in the wrong order.

**Easy Explanation:** Bubble sort is like bubbles rising in water — larger elements "bubble" to the end of the list.

**Roman Urdu:** Bubble sort mein adjacent elements compare karte hain aur agar ghalat order mein hon to swap karte hain. Bade elements end mein "bubble" ho jate hain.

**Process:**
1. Start from beginning of list
2. Compare each pair of adjacent elements
3. Swap if wrong order
4. Continue until no swaps needed

**Example:** List [5, 3, 8, 4, 2]
- Pass 1: [3, 5, 4, 2, 8] (8 bubbles to end)
- Pass 2: [3, 4, 2, 5, 8]
- Pass 3: [3, 2, 4, 5, 8]
- Pass 4: [2, 3, 4, 5, 8] (sorted)

**Roman Urdu:** List ko baar baar check karte hain. Har pass mein bada element end mein chala jata hai. Jab koi swap na ho to list sorted hai.

**Complexity:** O(n²) — inefficient for large datasets.

**Roman Urdu:** Bubble sort O(n²) time leta hai — bade datasets ke liye slow hai.

**Textbook Tidbit:** "While Bubble Sort is easy to implement, consider using more efficient sorting algorithms like Quick Sort or Merge Sort for larger datasets."

##### 3.6.1.2 Selection Sort

**Definition:** Selects the smallest (or largest) element from the unsorted part and swaps it with the first element of the unsorted part.

**Easy Explanation:** Selection sort finds the smallest item and puts it first, then finds the next smallest and puts it second, and so on.

**Roman Urdu:** Selection sort har baar sab se chhota element dhundh kar aage rakhta hai. Phir agla chhota, aur isi tarah.

**Process:**
1. Find minimum element in unsorted part
2. Swap with first unsorted element
3. Move boundary of sorted/unsorted
4. Repeat

**Example:** List [29, 10, 14, 37, 13]
- Pass 1: [10, 29, 14, 37, 13] (10 is smallest)
- Pass 2: [10, 13, 14, 37, 29] (13 is next smallest)
- Pass 3: [10, 13, 14, 37, 29] (14 already in place)
- Pass 4: [10, 13, 14, 29, 37] (sorted)

**Roman Urdu:** Har pass mein minimum dhundh kar aage rakhte hain.

**Complexity:** O(n²).

**Comparison: Bubble Sort vs. Selection Sort**

| Feature | Bubble Sort | Selection Sort |
|---------|-------------|----------------|
| Swaps | Many (every inversion) | Few (one per pass) |
| Complexity | O(n²) | O(n²) |
| Best for | Small data | Small data |
| Roman Urdu | Bohat swaps | Kam swaps |

---

#### 3.6.2 Search Algorithms

##### 3.6.2.1 Linear Search

**Definition:** Checks each item in a list one by one until the target is found.

**Easy Explanation:** Linear search is like looking for a specific book on a shelf by checking each book from left to right.

**Roman Urdu:** Linear search mein list ke har item ko ek ek karke check karte hain jab tak target na mil jaye. Jaise shelf par har kitaab check karna.

**Process:**
1. Start at beginning
2. Compare target with current item
3. If match, return position
4. If not, move to next
5. Repeat until found or end of list

**Example:** List [Karachi, Lahore, Islamabad, Faisalabad], search for "Islamabad"
- Check Karachi — not
- Check Lahore — not
- Check Islamabad — found!

**Roman Urdu:** Karachi check kiya, Lahore check kiya, Islamabad mil gaya.

**Complexity:** O(n) — linear time.

##### 3.6.2.2 Binary Search

**Definition:** Efficiently finds an item in a sorted list by repeatedly dividing the search interval in half.

**Easy Explanation:** Binary search is like looking up a word in a dictionary. You open the middle, see if your word is before or after, then repeat in that half.

**Roman Urdu:** Binary search mein sorted list ko baar baar aadha karte hain. Jaise dictionary mein beech se khol kar dekhte hain ke word pehle hai ya baad mein.

**Process:**
1. Find middle element
2. If target equals middle, return position
3. If target < middle, search left half
4. If target > middle, search right half
5. Repeat until found or interval empty

**Example:** Sorted list [1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13], search for 7
- Middle = 7 (index 3) — found!

**Roman Urdu:** Beech ka element 7 hai — mil gaya.

**Figure 3.6: Binary Search Process**
[Shows: Search 78 → Divide from middle (43) → Look right half → New mid (66) → Look right half → New mid (78) → Found]

**Explanation of Figure:**
- Start with sorted list [21, 34, 43, 57, 66, 78]
- Search for 78
- Middle = 43. 78 > 43, so search right half
- New middle = 66. 78 > 66, search right half
- New middle = 78. Found!

**Roman Urdu:** List [21, 34, 43, 57, 66, 78] mein 78 dhundhna. Beech 43 hai — 78 bara hai, right half dekho. Phir 66 — 78 bara hai, right half. Phir 78 mil gaya.

**Complexity:** O(log n) — much faster than linear search for large data.

**Roman Urdu:** Binary search O(log n) time leta hai — bade data ke liye linear search se bohat tez.

**Important:** Binary search only works on sorted lists.

**Textbook "Do You Know?":** "Binary Search is only effective on sorted lists. If your data isn't sorted, consider using a sorting algorithm like Merge Sort before applying Binary Search!"

**Roman Urdu:** Binary search sirf sorted list par kaam karta hai. Agar list sorted nahi hai to pehle sort karein.

**Comparison: Linear vs. Binary Search**

| Feature | Linear Search | Binary Search |
|---------|--------------|---------------|
| Requires sorted list | No | Yes |
| Complexity | O(n) | O(log n) |
| Speed | Slow for large data | Fast for large data |
| Method | Check each item | Divide and conquer |
| Roman Urdu | Har item check | Aadha karte jao |

---

#### 3.6.3 Graph Algorithms

**Definition:** Graph algorithms explore and analyze graphs — data structures made of nodes (vertices) connected by edges.

**Roman Urdu:** Graph algorithms graphs ko explore aur analyze karte hain — nodes aur edges se bane structures.

##### 3.6.3.1 Breadth-First Search (BFS)

**Definition:** Explores all nodes of a graph level by level, starting from a given node (root). Uses a queue.

**Easy Explanation:** BFS is like ripples in water — it explores all neighbors first, then neighbors of neighbors, and so on.

**Roman Urdu:** BFS level by level explore karta hai. Pehle root ke sab neighbors, phir unke neighbors, aur isi tarah. Jaise pani mein lehar.

**Process:**
1. Start from root, enqueue it
2. Dequeue a node, process it
3. Enqueue all unvisited neighbors
4. Repeat until queue empty

**Example:** Social network — find shortest path between two people (degrees of separation).

**Roman Urdu:** Social network mein do logon ke beech shortest path dhundhna.

**Complexity:** O(V + E) where V = vertices, E = edges.

##### 3.6.3.2 Depth-First Search (DFS)

**Definition:** Explores as far down a branch as possible before backtracking. Uses a stack.

**Easy Explanation:** DFS is like exploring a maze — go as deep as possible, and if you hit a dead end, backtrack and try another path.

**Roman Urdu:** DFS mein jitna deep ja sakte hain jate hain, phir wapas aa kar doosra raasta try karte hain. Jaise maze mein.

**Process:**
1. Start from root, push onto stack
2. Pop a node, process it
3. Push all unvisited neighbors onto stack
4. Repeat until stack empty

**Figure 3.7: Comparison of BFS and DFS**
[Shows BFS: Level 0 (0), Level 1 (1,2,3), Level 2 (4,5,6,7) — Output: 0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7]
[Shows DFS: 0 → 1 → 4 → 5 → 2 → 6 → 3 → 7 — Output: 0,1,4,5,2,6,3,7]

**Explanation of Figure 3.7:**
- **BFS:** Explores level by level. Output order: 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7
- **DFS:** Explores deep first. Output order: 0, 1, 4, 5, 2, 6, 3, 7

**Roman Urdu:** BFS level by level jata hai — 0, phir 1,2,3, phir 4,5,6,7. DFS deep jata hai — 0, phir 1, phir 4,5, phir 2,6, phir 3,7.

**Complexity:** O(V + E) — same as BFS.

**Comparison: BFS vs. DFS**

| Feature | BFS | DFS |
|---------|-----|-----|
| Data Structure | Queue | Stack |
| Exploration | Level by level | Deep first |
| Memory | More (stores many nodes) | Less (stores path) |
| Shortest path | Yes (unweighted) | No |
| Roman Urdu | Level by level | Deep pehle |

---

## C. Important Terminology

| English Term | Easy English Meaning | Roman Urdu Meaning |
|--------------|---------------------|-------------------|
| Computational Problem | Problem solvable by computer | Computer se solve hone wala masla |
| Algorithm | Step-by-step procedure | Step-by-step tareeqa |
| Decision Problem | Yes/No answer problem | Haan/nahi wala masla |
| Search Problem | Find an item | Item dhundhna |
| Optimization Problem | Find best solution | Best solution dhundhna |
| Counting Problem | Count possibilities | Possibilities ginna |
| Well-defined Problem | Clear goals and inputs | Clear masla |
| Ill-defined Problem | Vague goals | Ghalat clear masla |
| Generate-and-Test | Try all possibilities | Sab possibilities try karna |
| Solvable Problem | Algorithm exists | Algorithm maujood hai |
| Unsolvable Problem | No algorithm exists | Koi algorithm nahi |
| Halting Problem | Will program finish? | Program rukega ya nahi? |
| Tractable | Polynomial time | Manageable time |
| Intractable | Exponential time | Bohat zyada time |
| Class P | Easy problems | Aasaan problems |
| Class NP | Verifiable problems | Check hone wale problems |
| NP-Hard | Very hard problems | Bohat mushkil problems |
| NP-Complete | Hardest NP problems | Sab se mushkil NP problems |
| Time Complexity | Time grows with input | Input ke saath time |
| Space Complexity | Memory grows with input | Input ke saath memory |
| Big O Notation | Upper bound on time | Time ki upper limit |
| Divide and Conquer | Break and solve | Tod kar solve karna |
| Greedy Algorithm | Best local choice | Har step par best |
| Dynamic Programming | Store subproblem results | Subproblem results store |
| Backtracking | Try and go back | Try karo, wapas jao |
| Bubble Sort | Adjacent swap sort | Adjacent swap sorting |
| Selection Sort | Minimum first sort | Minimum pehle sorting |
| Linear Search | Check each item | Har item check |
| Binary Search | Divide and search | Aadha karke search |
| BFS | Level by level | Level by level |
| DFS | Deep first | Deep pehle |

---

## D. Differences and Comparisons

### Well-defined vs. Ill-defined Problems

| Feature | Well-defined | Ill-defined |
|---------|-------------|-------------|
| Goal | Clear | Vague |
| Input | Defined | Unclear |
| Process | Known | Unknown |
| Output | Specific | Ambiguous |
| Solvable by computer | Yes | Difficult/No |
| Roman Urdu | Sab clear | Kuch clear nahi |

### Tractable vs. Intractable Problems

| Feature | Tractable | Intractable |
|---------|-----------|-------------|
| Time | Polynomial | Exponential |
| Feasibility | Practical | Impractical |
| Class | P | NP-Hard, etc. |
| Example | Sorting | TSP |
| Roman Urdu | Manageable | Bohat zyada time |

### P vs. NP vs. NP-Hard vs. NP-Complete

| Class | Definition | Example | Roman Urdu |
|-------|-----------|---------|------------|
| P | Polynomial time solvable | Sorting | Jaldi solve |
| NP | Polynomial time verifiable | Sudoku | Jaldi check |
| NP-Hard | As hard as hardest NP | TSP | Bohat mushkil |
| NP-Complete | In NP and NP-Hard | Knapsack | Sab se mushkil NP |

### Bubble Sort vs. Selection Sort

| Feature | Bubble Sort | Selection Sort |
|---------|-------------|----------------|
| Method | Swap adjacent if wrong | Find minimum, swap with first |
| Swaps | Many | Few |
| Complexity | O(n²) | O(n²) |
| Roman Urdu | Adjacent swap | Minimum dhundh kar swap |

### Linear Search vs. Binary Search

| Feature | Linear Search | Binary Search |
|---------|--------------|---------------|
| Sorted list required | No | Yes |
| Complexity | O(n) | O(log n) |
| Method | Check each item | Divide and conquer |
| Speed | Slow | Fast |
| Roman Urdu | Har item check | Aadha karke search |

### BFS vs. DFS

| Feature | BFS | DFS |
|---------|-----|-----|
| Data Structure | Queue | Stack |
| Order | Level by level | Deep first |
| Memory | More | Less |
| Shortest path | Yes | No |
| Roman Urdu | Level by level | Deep pehle |

---

## E. Diagrams and Flowcharts

### Generate-and-Test Flowchart (Figure 3.2)

```
[Generator] → [Possible Solution] → [Tester] → [Correct?]
     ↑                                              |
     |____________ [Incorrect] _____________________|
                                                    |
                                            [Correct] → [Stop]
```

**Explanation:** Generator creates solutions. Tester checks. If correct, stop. If incorrect, generate another.

**Roman Urdu:** Generator solution banata hai. Tester check karta hai. Sahi ho to stop, ghalat ho to dobara.

### Binary Search Process (Figure 3.6)

```
List: [21, 34, 43, 57, 66, 78]
Search: 78
Step 1: mid = 43, 78 > 43 → search right
Step 2: mid = 66, 78 > 66 → search right
Step 3: mid = 78 → Found!
```

**Roman Urdu:** 78 dhundhna. Beech 43 — right. Phir 66 — right. Phir 78 — mil gaya.

### BFS vs. DFS (Figure 3.7)

**BFS:**
```
Level 0: 0
Level 1: 1, 2, 3
Level 2: 4, 5, 6, 7
Output: 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7
```

**DFS:**
```
Path: 0 → 1 → 4 → 5 → 2 → 6 → 3 → 7
Output: 0, 1, 4, 5, 2, 6, 3, 7
```

**Roman Urdu:** BFS level by level (0, 1,2,3, 4,5,6,7). DFS deep (0, 1, 4, 5, 2, 6, 3, 7).

### Complexity Classes (Figure 3.4)

```
        ┌─────────────┐
        │   NP-Hard   │
        │  ┌───────┐  │
        │  │NP-Comp│  │
        │  │┌─────┐│  │
        │  ││  NP ││  │
        │  ││┌───┐││  │
        │  │││ P │││  │
        │  ││└───┘││  │
        │  │└─────┘│  │
        │  └───────┘  │
        └─────────────┘
```

**Roman Urdu:** P sab se andar. NP mein P aur NP-Complete. NP-Hard NP-Complete par overlap karta hai.

---

## F. Python Programming

*Chapter 3 does not contain Python programs in the textbook. However, here are implementations of key algorithms:*

### Bubble Sort Implementation

```python
def bubble_sort(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(0, n - i - 1):
            if arr[j] > arr[j + 1]:
                arr[j], arr[j + 1] = arr[j + 1], arr[j]
    return arr

numbers = [5, 3, 8, 4, 2]
print("Original:", numbers)
print("Sorted:", bubble_sort(numbers))
```

**Output:**
```
Original: [5, 3, 8, 4, 2]
Sorted: [2, 3, 4, 5, 8]
```

**Explanation:** Outer loop runs n times. Inner loop compares adjacent elements and swaps if out of order.

**Roman Urdu:** Bubble sort mein adjacent elements compare karte hain aur swap karte hain.

### Binary Search Implementation

```python
def binary_search(arr, target):
    left, right = 0, len(arr) - 1
    while left <= right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if arr[mid] == target:
            return mid
        elif arr[mid] < target:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid - 1
    return -1

sorted_list = [1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13]
print(binary_search(sorted_list, 7))  # Output: 3
print(binary_search(sorted_list, 6))  # Output: -1
```

**Output:**
```
3
-1
```

**Explanation:** Binary search finds target by halving search space.

**Roman Urdu:** Binary search aadha karte hue target dhundhta hai.

## G. Important Formulas and Rules

### Big O Notation Summary

| Complexity | Name | Example |
|------------|------|---------|
| O(1) | Constant | Array access |
| O(log n) | Logarithmic | Binary search |
| O(n) | Linear | Linear search |
| O(n log n) | Linearithmic | Merge sort |
| O(n²) | Quadratic | Bubble sort |
| O(2ⁿ) | Exponential | TSP (brute force) |

**Roman Urdu:** Big O notation se pata chalta hai algorithm kitna time lega.

### Binary Search Midpoint Formula

\[
\text{mid} = \left\lfloor \frac{\text{left} + \text{right}}{2} \right\rfloor
\]

**Roman Urdu:** Binary search mein beech ka index nikalne ke liye left aur right ko jama karke 2 se divide karte hain.

## H. Textbook Exercise Solutions

### Multiple Choice Questions (MCQs)

**1. The characteristic of a well-defined problem is:**
a) Ambiguous goals and unclear requirements
b) Vague processes and inputs
c) Clear goals, inputs, processes, and outputs
d) Undefined solutions

**Answer: c) Clear goals, inputs, processes, and outputs**
**Explanation:** Well-defined problems have clear goals, defined inputs, known processes, and specific outputs.
**Roman Urdu:** Well-defined problem mein sab kuch clear hota hai — goal, input, process, output.

**2. Complexity class representing problems solvable efficiently by a deterministic algorithm:**
a) NP
b) NP-hard
c) NP-complete
d) P

**Answer: d) P**
**Explanation:** Class P contains problems solvable in polynomial time by a deterministic algorithm.
**Roman Urdu:** Class P wo problems hain jinhein efficiently solve kiya ja sakta hai.

**3. The statement that applies to unsolvable problems:**
a) They can be solved in polynomial time
b) They cannot be solved by any algorithm
c) They are always in NP class
d) They require exponential time to solve

**Answer: b) They cannot be solved by any algorithm**
**Explanation:** Unsolvable problems have no algorithm that can solve them for all cases.
**Roman Urdu:** Unsolvable problems ko koi algorithm solve nahi kar sakta.

**4. The meaning of NP in computational complexity is:**
a) Non-deterministic Polynomial time
b) Negative Polynomial time
c) Non-trivial Polynomial time
d) Numerical Polynomial time

**Answer: a) Non-deterministic Polynomial time**
**Explanation:** NP stands for Non-deterministic Polynomial time.
**Roman Urdu:** NP ka matlab Non-deterministic Polynomial time hai.

**5. Search algorithm more efficient for large datasets:**
a) Bubble Sort
b) Merge Sort
c) Selection Sort
d) Quick Sort

**Answer: b) Merge Sort** (Note: The question asks for a search algorithm but lists sorting algorithms. Among these, Merge Sort is most efficient for large datasets with O(n log n).)
**Explanation:** Merge Sort has O(n log n) complexity, better than O(n²) of Bubble and Selection Sort.
**Roman Urdu:** Merge Sort O(n log n) time leta hai — bade datasets ke liye efficient.

**6. A scenario where Dynamic Programming proves most useful:**
a) Problems without overlapping subproblems
b) Problems solved by making local choices
c) Problems with overlapping subproblems and optimal substructure
d) Problems divided into independent subproblems

**Answer: c) Problems with overlapping subproblems and optimal substructure**
**Explanation:** DP is useful when subproblems repeat (overlapping) and optimal solution can be built from optimal subproblems.
**Roman Urdu:** DP tab useful hai jab subproblems repeat hon aur optimal substructure ho.

**7. An algorithm that sorts data by stepping through the list and swapping adjacent elements if needed is:**
a) Selection Sort
b) Quick Sort
c) Bubble Sort
d) Merge Sort

**Answer: c) Bubble Sort**
**Explanation:** Bubble Sort compares adjacent elements and swaps them if in wrong order.
**Roman Urdu:** Bubble Sort adjacent elements compare karke swap karta hai.

**8. Time complexity of Depth-First Search (DFS) in a graph is:**
a) O(n log n)
b) O(V)
c) O(V + E)
d) O(n)

**Answer: c) O(V + E)**
**Explanation:** DFS visits each vertex and edge once, so O(V + E).
**Roman Urdu:** DFS O(V + E) time leta hai — har vertex aur edge ek baar.

**9. Best description of time complexity:**
a) Amount of memory an algorithm needs
b) Time taken as a function of input size
c) Efficiency as input size grows
d) Upper bound of space requirements

**Answer: b) Time taken as a function of input size**
**Explanation:** Time complexity describes how runtime grows with input size.
**Roman Urdu:** Time complexity batati hai ke input barhne par time kaise barhta hai.

**10. An algorithm with a time complexity of O(n log n):**
a) Bubble Sort
b) Binary Search
c) Merge Sort
d) Insertion Sort

**Answer: c) Merge Sort**
**Explanation:** Merge Sort has O(n log n) complexity.
**Roman Urdu:** Merge Sort O(n log n) time leta hai.

---

### Short Questions

**1. Differentiate between well-defined and ill-defined problems within the realm of computational problem-solving.**

**Answer:** Well-defined problems have clear goals, defined inputs, known processes, and specific outputs — they can be solved by algorithms (e.g., checking if a number is even). Ill-defined problems lack clear definitions, have vague goals, and are difficult to solve computationally (e.g., reducing poverty).

**Roman Urdu:** Well-defined problems mein sab clear hota hai. Ill-defined problems mein goal vague hota hai aur computer se solve karna mushkil hota hai.

**2. Outline the main steps involved in the Generate-and-Test method.**

**Answer:** (1) Generate a possible solution. (2) Test if it meets requirements. (3) If correct, stop. (4) If incorrect, generate another solution. (5) Repeat until solution found or all possibilities exhausted.

**Roman Urdu:** Solution generate karo, test karo, sahi ho to stop, ghalat ho to dobara generate karo.

**3. Compare tractable and intractable problems in the context of computational complexity.**

**Answer:** Tractable problems can be solved in polynomial time (e.g., sorting, O(n log n)) and are practical for large inputs. Intractable problems require exponential time (e.g., TSP) and become impractical as input grows.

**Roman Urdu:** Tractable problems polynomial time mein solve ho jate hain — practical. Intractable problems exponential time lete hain — bade inputs ke liye impractical.

**4. Summarize the key idea behind Greedy Algorithms.**

**Answer:** Greedy algorithms make the locally optimal choice at each step, hoping to find a globally optimal solution. They are fast and simple but do not always guarantee the best overall solution.

**Roman Urdu:** Greedy algorithms har step par best local choice karte hain — fast aur simple, lekin hamesha best overall solution guarantee nahi karte.

**5. Discuss the advantages of using Dynamic Programming.**

**Answer:** DP avoids redundant calculations by storing subproblem results, reducing time complexity. It is useful for problems with overlapping subproblems and optimal substructure (e.g., Fibonacci, knapsack). It trades memory for speed.

**Roman Urdu:** DP subproblem results store karta hai — redundant calculations kam hoti hain. Memory zyada leta hai lekin speed behtar hoti hai.

**6. Compare the advantages of Breadth-First Search (BFS) with Depth-First Search (DFS) in graph traversal.**

**Answer:** BFS explores level by level and finds shortest paths in unweighted graphs. DFS explores deep first and uses less memory for deep graphs. BFS uses a queue; DFS uses a stack.

**Roman Urdu:** BFS level by level jata hai aur shortest path dhundhta hai. DFS deep jata hai aur memory kam leta hai.

**7. Explain the importance of breaking down a problem into smaller components in algorithmic thinking.**

**Answer:** Breaking down problems makes them easier to understand and solve. Each smaller component can be solved independently, and solutions can be combined. This is the basis of divide-and-conquer and modular programming.

**Roman Urdu:** Problem ko chhote hisson mein todne se samajhna aur solve karna asaan hota hai. Har hissa alag solve karke mila sakte hain.

**8. Identify the key factors used to evaluate the performance of an algorithm.**

**Answer:** Time complexity (how runtime grows with input), space complexity (how memory usage grows), and scalability (how well it handles large inputs). Big O notation is used to express these.

**Roman Urdu:** Algorithm ki performance time complexity, space complexity, aur scalability se evaluate hoti hai.

---

### Long Questions

**1. Provide a detailed explanation of why the Halting Problem is considered unsolvable and its implications in computer science.**

**Answer:** The Halting Problem asks: given a program and its input, will the program eventually halt (finish) or run forever? Alan Turing proved in 1936 that no general algorithm can solve this for all possible program-input pairs. The proof uses contradiction: assume a program H that solves the Halting Problem. Then construct a program P that does the opposite of what H predicts — if H says P halts, P loops forever; if H says P loops, P halts. This contradiction shows H cannot exist. Implications: Some problems are fundamentally unsolvable by computers; we cannot automate all verification tasks; it defines the limits of computation.

**Roman Urdu:** Halting problem poochta hai ke program rukega ya nahi. Turing ne prove kiya ke koi algorithm isse solve nahi kar sakta. Iska matlab hai ke kuch problems computer se solve nahi ho sakti — computation ki limits hain.

**2. Discuss the characteristics of search problems and compare the efficiency of Linear Search and Binary Search algorithm.**

**Answer:** Search problems involve finding an item that meets certain criteria (e.g., finding a student by ID). Linear Search checks each item one by one — O(n) time, works on unsorted data. Binary Search repeatedly divides a sorted list in half — O(log n) time, much faster for large data but requires sorted input. Binary Search is preferred for large sorted datasets; Linear Search is simpler and works on any data.

**Roman Urdu:** Search problems mein item dhundhna hota hai. Linear search har item check karta hai — O(n). Binary search aadha karte jata hai — O(log n), lekin sorted list chahiye.

**3. Discuss the nature of optimization problems and provide examples of their applications in real-world scenarios.**

**Answer:** Optimization problems seek the best solution according to some criteria (e.g., shortest path, maximum profit, minimum cost). Examples: Traveling Salesman Problem (shortest route), Knapsack Problem (maximum value within weight limit), route planning in GPS, resource allocation in business, portfolio optimization in finance. These problems are often NP-Hard, meaning no efficient algorithm is known for large inputs.

**Roman Urdu:** Optimization problems mein best solution dhundhna hota hai — jaise shortest route, maximum profit. TSP, Knapsack, GPS routing examples hain. Yeh often NP-Hard hote hain.

**4. Explain the process and time complexity of the Bubble Sort algorithm. Compare it with another sorting algorithm of your choice in terms of efficiency.**

**Answer:** Bubble Sort repeatedly compares adjacent elements and swaps them if out of order. After each pass, the largest unsorted element "bubbles" to the end. Time complexity: O(n²) — inefficient for large data. Compare with Merge Sort: Merge Sort divides the list, sorts halves, and merges — O(n log n). Merge Sort is much faster for large datasets. Bubble Sort is simple and good for learning but not practical for large data.

**Roman Urdu:** Bubble Sort adjacent elements compare karke swap karta hai — O(n²). Merge Sort divide karke sort karta hai — O(n log n), bade data ke liye behtar.

**5. Discuss the differences between time complexity and space complexity. How do they impact the choice of an algorithm for a specific problem?**

**Answer:** Time complexity measures how runtime grows with input size; space complexity measures how memory usage grows. An algorithm may be fast but use lots of memory (e.g., Merge Sort uses O(n) extra space), or slow but memory-efficient (e.g., Bubble Sort uses O(1) extra space). The choice depends on constraints: if memory is limited, choose a memory-efficient algorithm; if speed is critical, choose a faster algorithm even if it uses more memory.

**Roman Urdu:** Time complexity time batati hai, space complexity memory. Algorithm choose karte waqt dono dekhte hain — agar memory kam ho to memory-efficient algorithm chunein, agar speed zaroori ho to fast algorithm.

### Frequently Confused Concepts

| Concept 1 | Concept 2 | Key Difference |
|-----------|-----------|----------------|
| P | NP | Solvable vs. Verifiable |
| NP-Hard | NP-Complete | At least as hard vs. In NP and hard |
| BFS | DFS | Queue/level vs. Stack/deep |
| Linear Search | Binary Search | Unsorted/check all vs. Sorted/halve |
| Bubble Sort | Selection Sort | Adjacent swap vs. Minimum swap |
| Tractable | Intractable | Polynomial vs. Exponential |
| Time Complexity | Space Complexity | Time vs. Memory |
| Greedy | Dynamic Programming | Local choice vs. Stored results |

### Quick Revision Notes

- **Computational Problem:** Input + Process + Output
- **Problem Types:** Decision, Search, Optimization, Counting
- **Well-defined:** Clear goals/inputs/processes/outputs
- **Generate-and-Test:** Try all possibilities
- **Solvable vs. Unsolvable:** Algorithm exists vs. no algorithm
- **Halting Problem:** Unsolvable — cannot predict if program halts
- **Tractable:** Polynomial (P); **Intractable:** Exponential
- **Complexity Classes:** P (easy), NP (verifiable), NP-Hard (very hard), NP-Complete (hardest NP)
- **Big O:** O(1) < O(log n) < O(n) < O(n log n) < O(n²) < O(2ⁿ)
- **Divide and Conquer:** Break, solve, combine
- **Greedy:** Best local choice
- **Dynamic Programming:** Store subproblem results
- **Backtracking:** Try, if fail, go back
- **Bubble Sort:** Adjacent swaps, O(n²)
- **Selection Sort:** Minimum first, O(n²)
- **Linear Search:** Check each, O(n)
- **Binary Search:** Halve, O(log n), requires sorted
- **BFS:** Queue, level by level, O(V+E)
- **DFS:** Stack, deep first, O(V+E)

**Computational Problem:** Input → Process → Output.
**Types:** Decision, Search, Optimization, Counting.
**Well-defined:** Clear everything. **Ill-defined:** Vague.
**Generate-and-Test:** Try all possibilities until correct.
**Solvable:** Algorithm exists. **Unsolvable:** No algorithm (Halting Problem).
**Tractable:** Polynomial (P). **Intractable:** Exponential.
**P:** Easy to solve. **NP:** Easy to verify. **NP-Hard:** Very hard. **NP-Complete:** Hardest NP.
**Big O:** O(1), O(log n), O(n), O(n log n), O(n²), O(2ⁿ).
**Divide and Conquer:** Break → Solve → Combine.
**Greedy:** Best local choice.
**DP:** Store subproblem results.
**Backtracking:** Try, fail, go back.
**Bubble Sort:** Adjacent swaps, O(n²).
**Selection Sort:** Minimum first, O(n²).
**Linear Search:** Check each, O(n).
**Binary Search:** Halve, O(log n), sorted required.
**BFS:** Queue, level by level.
**DFS:** Stack, deep first.

### Important Definitions

1. **Computational Problem:** Challenge solvable by computer with input, process, output.
2. **Algorithm:** Step-by-step procedure to solve a problem.
3. **Decision Problem:** Yes/No answer problem.
4. **Search Problem:** Find an item meeting criteria.
5. **Optimization Problem:** Find best solution.
6. **Counting Problem:** Count possibilities.
7. **Well-defined Problem:** Clear goals, inputs, processes, outputs.
8. **Ill-defined Problem:** Vague goals and requirements.
9. **Generate-and-Test:** Try all possible solutions.
10. **Solvable Problem:** Algorithm exists within finite time.
11. **Unsolvable Problem:** No algorithm can solve for all cases.
12. **Halting Problem:** Determine if program halts — unsolvable.
13. **Tractable Problem:** Solvable in polynomial time.
14. **Intractable Problem:** Requires exponential time.
15. **Class P:** Polynomial time solvable.
16. **Class NP:** Polynomial time verifiable.
17. **NP-Hard:** At least as hard as hardest NP.
18. **NP-Complete:** In NP and NP-Hard.
19. **Time Complexity:** Runtime growth with input size.
20. **Space Complexity:** Memory growth with input size.
21. **Big O Notation:** Upper bound on time complexity.
22. **Divide and Conquer:** Break problem, solve parts, combine.
23. **Greedy Algorithm:** Best local choice at each step.
24. **Dynamic Programming:** Store subproblem results.
25. **Backtracking:** Try path, backtrack if fails.
26. **Bubble Sort:** Adjacent swap sorting, O(n²).
27. **Selection Sort:** Minimum selection sorting, O(n²).
28. **Linear Search:** Check each item, O(n).
29. **Binary Search:** Halve search space, O(log n).
30. **BFS:** Level-by-level graph traversal using queue.
31. **DFS:** Deep-first graph traversal using stack.

### Important Differences

| Comparison | Key Difference |
|------------|----------------|
| Well-defined vs. Ill-defined | Clear vs. Vague |
| Tractable vs. Intractable | Polynomial vs. Exponential |
| P vs. NP | Solve vs. Verify |
| Bubble vs. Selection Sort | Adjacent swap vs. Minimum swap |
| Linear vs. Binary Search | Check all vs. Halve |
| BFS vs. DFS | Level vs. Deep |
| Greedy vs. DP | Local choice vs. Stored results |

# CHAPTER 4: COMPUTATIONAL STRUCTURES

## A. Chapter Introduction

**Chapter Overview:** This chapter introduces the fundamental data structures used in computer science — lists, stacks, queues, trees, and graphs. You will learn how data is organized, stored, and accessed efficiently. These structures are the building blocks of all software: databases, operating systems, websites, and games all rely on them.

**Learning Objectives (from textbook SLOs):**
- Define and explain primitive computational structures (lists, stacks, queues, trees, graphs)
- Identify and describe characteristics and properties of different structures
- Perform basic operations (insertion, deletion, traversal, searching)
- Understand and implement LIFO (Last-In, First-Out) and FIFO (First-In, First-Out) principles
- Compare and contrast different types of trees and graphs
- Analyze and choose the most suitable structure based on problem requirements
- Apply computational structures in real-world scenarios
- Combine different structures to solve complex problems

**Why This Chapter Is Important:** Choosing the right data structure makes programs faster, simpler, and more reliable. For example, a queue is perfect for managing print jobs, while a stack is ideal for undo operations in software. Understanding these structures is essential for programming, algorithms, and software design.

**Roman Urdu:** Yeh chapter batata hai ke data ko computer mein kaise organize karte hain — lists, stacks, queues, trees, aur graphs. Sahi structure chunna program ko tez aur asaan banata hai. Jaise printer queue ke liye queue structure, aur undo ke liye stack structure.

**Real-Life Applications:** Browser history (stack), printer queue (queue), file systems (tree), social networks (graph), to-do lists (list).

---

## B. Complete Topic-Wise Notes

### 4.1 Primitive Computational Structures

**Definition:** Primitive computational structures are the basic ways of organizing and storing data in computer memory.

**Roman Urdu:** Primitive computational structures data ko computer memory mein organize aur store karne ke basic tareeqe hain.

#### 4.1.1 Lists

**Definition:** A list is a data structure used to store multiple pieces of data in a specific sequence. Each piece of data (element) is positioned at a particular index, allowing easy access and management.

**Easy Explanation:** A list is like a shopping list — items are written one after another in order. You can add items, remove items, and find items by their position.

**Roman Urdu:** List ek shopping list jaisi hai — items ek ke baad ek order mein hote hain. Aap items add, remove, aur position se dhundh sakte hain.

##### 4.1.1.1 List Creation

In Python, lists are created using square brackets `[]`, with items separated by commas.

**Example:**
```python
items = ["Decorations", "Snacks", "Cold drinks", "Plates", "Balloons"]
print(items)
```

**Output:**
```
['Decorations', 'Snacks', 'Cold drinks', 'Plates', 'Balloons']
```

**Explanation:** `items` is the list name. Each item is enclosed in quotes (for text) and separated by commas.

**Roman Urdu:** `items` list ka naam hai. Har item quotes mein hai aur comma se separate hai.

##### 4.1.1.2 List Properties

**1. Dynamic Size**
- A list can change its size — you can add or remove items
- The list automatically adjusts

**Roman Urdu:** List ka size change ho sakta hai — items add ya remove kar sakte hain. List khud adjust karti hai.

**2. Index-Based Access**
- Every item has a position (index)
- First item has index 0, second has index 1, and so on
- Use indexes to get specific items

**Roman Urdu:** Har item ka ek index hota hai. Pehla item index 0, doosra index 1. Index se item access karte hain.

**3. Ordered Collection**
- The order in which items are added is preserved
- First added item stays in its position unless changed

**Roman Urdu:** Items ka order preserve rehta hai. Pehle add kiya hua item apni jagah rehta hai.

##### 4.1.1.3 List Operations

**1. Insertion:**
Adding a new item to the list. Use `insert()` to add at a specific position.

```python
party_list = ["Buy drinks", "Buy decorations", "Buy snacks", "Buy cold drinks"]
party_list.insert(0, "Invite friends")
print(party_list)
```

**Output:**
```
['Invite friends', 'Buy drinks', 'Buy decorations', 'Buy snacks', 'Buy cold drinks']
```

**Explanation:** `insert(0, "Invite friends")` adds "Invite friends" at index 0 (the beginning).

**Roman Urdu:** `insert(0, "Invite friends")` se "Invite friends" list ke shuru mein add hota hai.

**2. Deletion:**
Removing an item from the list.

**Removing by Value:**
```python
party_list = ["Invite friends", "Buy decorations", "Buy snacks", "Buy cold drinks"]
party_list.remove("Buy snacks")
print(party_list)
```

**Output:**
```
['Invite friends', 'Buy decorations', 'Buy cold drinks']
```

**Explanation:** `remove("Buy snacks")` deletes the first occurrence of "Buy snacks".

**Roman Urdu:** `remove()` se specific item delete hota hai.

**3. Searching:**
Finding an item in the list using the `in` keyword.

```python
party_list = ["Invite friends", "Buy decorations", "Buy cold drinks"]
if "Buy cold drinks" in party_list:
    print("Buy cold drinks is on the list.")
else:
    print("Buy cold drinks is not on the list.")
```

**Output:**
```
Buy cold drinks is on the list.
```

**Explanation:** `in` checks if an item exists in the list.

**Roman Urdu:** `in` keyword check karta hai ke item list mein maujood hai ya nahi.

##### 4.1.1.4 Applications of Lists

**1. Data Storage and Manipulation:**
- Store and manage collections of data (records, entries, values)
- Easy insertion, deletion, and access

**Roman Urdu:** Lists data store aur manage karne ke liye use hoti hain — records, entries, values.

**2. Stack and Queue Implementations:**
- Lists can implement stacks (LIFO) and queues (FIFO)
- Fundamental for algorithms and tasks

**Roman Urdu:** Lists se stack aur queue bhi banaye ja sakte hain.

---

#### 4.1.2 Stacks

**Definition:** A stack is a data structure where you can only add or remove items from one end, called the "top". It operates on the Last-In, First-Out (LIFO) principle.

**Easy Explanation:** A stack is like a stack of books. You can only take the top book off, and you can only put a new book on top. The last book placed is the first one removed.

**Roman Urdu:** Stack kitaabon ke dher jaisa hai. Sirf upar se kitaab nikal sakte hain aur upar hi rakh sakte hain. Jo aakhri mein rakhi gayi, wo pehle nikalti hai. Isse LIFO kehte hain.

**Figure 4.1: Stack of Books** — Shows books stacked on top of each other.

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 4.1 mein kitaabon ka dher dikhaya hai — sirf upar wali kitaab accessible hai.

##### 4.1.2.1 Stack Operations

**Push:** Adding an item to the top of the stack.
**Pop:** Removing the item from the top of the stack.

**Python Example:**
```python
stack_of_books = []
print("Initial stack:", stack_of_books)

# Push operation
stack_of_books.append("Book A")
print("Stack after pushing 'Book A':", stack_of_books)

stack_of_books.append("Book B")
print("Stack after pushing 'Book B':", stack_of_books)

# Pop operation
top_book = stack_of_books.pop()
print("Removed book:", top_book)
print("Stack after popping the top book:", stack_of_books)
```

**Output:**
```
Initial stack: []
Stack after pushing 'Book A': ['Book A']
Stack after pushing 'Book B': ['Book A', 'Book B']
Removed book: Book B
Stack after popping the top book: ['Book A']
```

**Explanation:** The code creates an empty stack. It adds "Book A" and "Book B" to the top. Then it removes the top book ("Book B"), showing that the last added is the first removed.

**Roman Urdu:** Khali stack banaya. "Book A" aur "Book B" push kiye. Phir pop kiya — "Book B" nikla kyunki wo aakhri mein add hua tha (LIFO).

**Comparison: Stack vs. Queue**

| Feature | Stack | Queue |
|---------|-------|-------|
| Principle | LIFO (Last-In, First-Out) | FIFO (First-In, First-Out) |
| Access | Only from top | From front (remove) and back (add) |
| Operations | Push, Pop | Enqueue, Dequeue |
| Example | Undo button, browser history | Printer queue, ticket line |
| Roman Urdu | Aakhri pehle | Pehla pehle |

---

#### 4.1.3 Queues

**Definition:** A queue is a data structure that follows the First-In, First-Out (FIFO) principle. The first item added is the first one removed.

**Easy Explanation:** A queue is like a line at a bank. The first person in line is served first. You add to the back and remove from the front.

**Roman Urdu:** Queue bank ki line jaisi hai. Pehla aadmi pehle serve hota hai. Peeche se add karte hain, aage se remove karte hain. Isse FIFO kehte hain.

**Figure 4.2: Queue of Persons in Front of the Bank** — Shows people standing in a line.

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 4.2 mein bank ke bahar logon ki line dikhayi hai — pehla aadmi pehle jayega.

##### 4.1.3.1 Queue Operations

**Enqueue:** Add an item to the back of the queue.
**Dequeue:** Remove an item from the front of the queue.

**Python Example:**
```python
from queue import Queue

q = Queue()

# Enqueue
q.put("Ahmed")
q.put("Fatima")

# Peek (view front without removing)
front_person = q.queue[0]
print(front_person)  # Output: Ahmed

# Dequeue
removed_person = q.get()
print(removed_person)  # Output: Ahmed

# Enqueue another
q.put("Sara")

# View updated queue
updated_queue = list(q.queue)
print(updated_queue)  # Output: ['Fatima', 'Sara']
```

**Output:**
```
Ahmed
Ahmed
['Fatima', 'Sara']
```

**Explanation:** The code manages a line of people. It adds Ahmed and Fatima to the queue. It checks the front person (Ahmed) without removing. It removes Ahmed (dequeue). Then adds Sara and shows the updated queue.

**Roman Urdu:** Queue mein Ahmed aur Fatima add kiye. Front dekha (Ahmed). Ahmed remove kiya. Sara add ki. Updated queue dikhaya.

---

#### 4.1.4 Trees

**Definition:** A tree is a data structure that organizes information in a hierarchical way, starting from a root node and branching out to child nodes.

**Easy Explanation:** A tree is like a family tree or a company organization chart. One main person (root) branches out to children, who branch out further.

**Roman Urdu:** Tree ek family tree ya organization chart jaisa hai. Ek main node (root) hota hai jo branches mein phailta hai — children, grandchildren, waghera.

**Figure 4.3: Family Tree** — Shows grandfather/grandmother at top, then father/mother/uncle/aunt, then children.

**Roman Urdu:** Figure 4.3 mein family tree dikhaya hai — dada/dadi upar, phir walid/khala/chacha, phir bachay.

##### 4.1.4.1 Properties of Trees

**1. Root Node:**
- The topmost node in a tree
- Like the main folder in a computer

**Roman Urdu:** Root node sab se upar hota hai. Jaise computer mein main folder.

**2. Edges and Nodes:**
- Nodes are individual elements
- Edges connect nodes
- A node without children is called a leaf

**Roman Urdu:** Nodes individual elements hain. Edges unhein connect karte hain. Jis node ke koi children na hon use leaf kehte hain.

**3. Height:**
- The longest path from root to the farthest leaf
- Tells how deep the tree is

**Roman Urdu:** Height batati hai ke tree kitna gehra hai — root se sab se door leaf tak ka raasta.

**4. Balanced Trees:**
- A tree is balanced if left and right branches are nearly the same height

**Roman Urdu:** Balanced tree mein left aur right branches ki height lagbhag barabar hoti hai.

##### 4.1.4.2 Applications of Trees

**1. File Systems:**
- Pre-order traversal for creating backups — root first, then contents

**Roman Urdu:** File systems mein backup ke liye pre-order traversal use hota hai — pehle root, phir contents.

**2. File System Deletion:**
- Post-order traversal ensures files and directories deleted in correct order — children before parent

**Roman Urdu:** File deletion mein post-order traversal — pehle children delete, phir parent.

**3. Hierarchical Data Representation:**
- Organizational charts, family trees

**Roman Urdu:** Hierarchical data — organization charts, family trees.

**4. Decision Making:**
- Decision trees used in algorithms to make decisions based on conditions

**Roman Urdu:** Decision trees mein conditions ke hisaab se decisions liye jate hain.

---

#### 4.1.5 Introduction to Graphs

**Definition:** A graph is a data structure consisting of vertices (nodes) connected by edges. It represents networks of connections.

**Easy Explanation:** A graph is like a map of cities connected by roads. Cities are vertices, roads are edges. Unlike a tree, a graph has no single root and can have cycles.

**Roman Urdu:** Graph ek map jaisa hai — cities vertices hain, roads edges hain. Tree ki tarah koi single root nahi hota, aur cycles bhi ho sakti hain.

**Example:** Social network — people are vertices, friendships are edges.

**Roman Urdu:** Social network mein log vertices hain, dosti edges hain.

**Difference from a Tree:**

| Feature | Tree | Graph |
|---------|------|-------|
| Root | Single root | No root |
| Cycles | No cycles | Cycles allowed |
| Hierarchy | Hierarchical | Not necessarily hierarchical |
| Paths | Exactly one path between nodes | Multiple paths possible |
| Roman Urdu | Ek root, koi cycle nahi | Koi root nahi, cycles ho sakti hain |

##### 4.1.5.1 Characteristics of Graphs

Graphs have several defining features:
- **Vertices (Nodes):** The entities in the graph
- **Edges:** Connections between vertices
- **Degree:** Number of edges connected to a vertex
- **Weight:** Value associated with an edge (distance, cost)
- **Direction:** Edges can be directed (one-way) or undirected (two-way)

**Roman Urdu:** Vertices nodes hain. Edges connections hain. Degree batata hai kitne edges jude hain. Weight edge ki value hai. Direction batati hai edge one-way hai ya two-way.

##### 4.1.5.2 Properties of Graphs

**Degree:** Number of edges connected to a vertex.
- Example: If a city is connected to three other cities, its degree is 3.

**Weight:** Value on an edge representing distance, cost, or time.
- Example: A road between two cities is 50 km long — weight = 50.

**Direction:** Edges can be directed or undirected.
- Directed: One-way connection
- Undirected: Two-way connection

**Roman Urdu:** Degree = kitne edges jude hain. Weight = edge ki value (distance, cost). Direction = one-way ya two-way.

##### 4.1.5.3 Types of Graphs

**1. Directed Graphs:**
- Edges have direction (one-way)
- Example: One-way streets in a city

**Figure 4.4: Directed Weighted Graph** — Shows arrows between vertices with weights.

**Roman Urdu:** Directed graph mein edges ki direction hoti hai — jaise one-way street. Figure 4.4 mein arrows ke saath weights dikhaye hain.

**2. Undirected Graphs:**
- Edges have no direction (two-way)
- Example: Friendship — if A is friends with B, B is friends with A

**Figure 4.5: Undirected Graph** — Shows lines (no arrows) between vertices.

**Roman Urdu:** Undirected graph mein direction nahi hoti — jaise dosti. Figure 4.5 mein simple lines hain.

**3. Weighted Graphs:**
- Each edge has a weight (cost, distance, time)
- Example: City map with distances between landmarks

**Roman Urdu:** Weighted graph mein har edge ka weight hota hai — jaise distances.

**Comparison: Directed vs. Undirected**

| Feature | Directed | Undirected |
|---------|----------|------------|
| Direction | One-way | Two-way |
| Edge representation | Arrow | Line |
| Example | One-way street | Friendship |
| Roman Urdu | One-way | Two-way |

**Comparison: Weighted vs. Unweighted**

| Feature | Weighted | Unweighted |
|---------|----------|------------|
| Edge value | Has weight | No weight |
| Example | Distance map | Social network |
| Roman Urdu | Weight hota hai | Weight nahi hota |

---

## C. Important Terminology

| English Term | Easy English Meaning | Roman Urdu Meaning |
|--------------|---------------------|-------------------|
| List | Ordered collection of items | Items ka ordered collection |
| Stack | LIFO data structure | LIFO structure |
| Queue | FIFO data structure | FIFO structure |
| Push | Add to stack top | Stack ke top par add |
| Pop | Remove from stack top | Stack ke top se remove |
| Enqueue | Add to queue back | Queue ke peeche add |
| Dequeue | Remove from queue front | Queue ke aage se remove |
| LIFO | Last-In, First-Out | Aakhri pehle |
| FIFO | First-In, First-Out | Pehla pehle |
| Tree | Hierarchical data structure | Hierarchical structure |
| Root | Top node of tree | Tree ka upar wala node |
| Node | Individual element | Individual element |
| Edge | Connection between nodes | Nodes ke beech connection |
| Leaf | Node without children | Bina children wala node |
| Height | Longest path to leaf | Leaf tak sab se lamba raasta |
| Balanced Tree | Left and right nearly equal | Left right barabar |
| Graph | Vertices connected by edges | Vertices edges se jude |
| Vertex | Node in graph | Graph ka node |
| Directed Graph | One-way edges | One-way edges |
| Undirected Graph | Two-way edges | Two-way edges |
| Weighted Graph | Edges have values | Edges ki values hain |
| Degree | Number of edges at vertex | Vertex par kitne edges |

---

## D. Differences and Comparisons

### List vs. Stack vs. Queue

| Feature | List | Stack | Queue |
|---------|------|-------|-------|
| Access | Any position | Top only | Front (remove), back (add) |
| Principle | Ordered | LIFO | FIFO |
| Operations | Insert, remove, search | Push, pop | Enqueue, dequeue |
| Example | Shopping list | Undo button | Printer queue |
| Roman Urdu | Kisi bhi position | Sirf top | Aage se remove, peeche se add |

### Stack vs. Queue

| Feature | Stack | Queue |
|---------|-------|-------|
| Principle | LIFO | FIFO |
| Add | Push (top) | Enqueue (back) |
| Remove | Pop (top) | Dequeue (front) |
| Example | Browser history | Ticket line |
| Roman Urdu | Aakhri pehle | Pehla pehle |

### Tree vs. Graph

| Feature | Tree | Graph |
|---------|------|-------|
| Root | Single root | No root |
| Cycles | No | Yes (possible) |
| Hierarchy | Hierarchical | Not necessarily |
| Paths | Exactly one | Multiple |
| Roman Urdu | Ek root, no cycle | Koi root nahi, cycles possible |

### Directed vs. Undirected Graph

| Feature | Directed | Undirected |
|---------|----------|------------|
| Direction | One-way | Two-way |
| Edge | Arrow | Line |
| Example | One-way street | Friendship |
| Roman Urdu | One-way | Two-way |

### Weighted vs. Unweighted Graph

| Feature | Weighted | Unweighted |
|---------|----------|------------|
| Edge value | Has weight | No weight |
| Example | Distance map | Social network |
| Roman Urdu | Weight hota hai | Weight nahi hota |

## E. Diagrams and Flowcharts

### Stack of Books (Figure 4.1)

```
    [Book C]  ← Top (accessible)
    [Book B]
    [Book A]  ← Bottom
```

**Explanation:** Only the top book can be removed (pop) or a new book added (push). LIFO.

**Roman Urdu:** Sirf upar wali kitaab nikal ya rakh sakte hain. LIFO.

### Queue of Persons (Figure 4.2)

```
Front → [Person 1] [Person 2] [Person 3] ← Back
         (served first)          (added last)
```

**Explanation:** Person 1 is served first. New persons join at the back. FIFO.

**Roman Urdu:** Person 1 pehle serve hoga. Naye log peeche aate hain. FIFO.

### Family Tree (Figure 4.3)

```
        [Grandfather/Grandmother]
              /            \
      [Father]           [Uncle]
      /      \           /      \
  [Brother] [Sister] [Cousin] [Cousin]
```

**Explanation:** Root at top. Children branch out. Leaves at bottom.

**Roman Urdu:** Root upar. Children branches mein. Leaves neeche.

### Directed Weighted Graph (Figure 4.4)

```
    A --10--> B
    |         |
    5         15
    ↓         ↓
    C --20--> D
```

**Explanation:** Arrows show direction. Numbers show weights.

**Roman Urdu:** Arrows direction dikhate hain. Numbers weights hain.

### Undirected Graph (Figure 4.5)

```
    A ------ B
    |        |
    |        |
    C ------ D
```

**Explanation:** Lines (no arrows) show two-way connections.

**Roman Urdu:** Lines (arrows nahi) two-way connections dikhati hain.
